Trading Analysis Report: AVGO¶

Generated: 2026-06-30 10:28:29

I. Analyst Team Reports¶

Market Analyst¶

Now I have all the data needed for a comprehensive analysis. Let me compile the findings.

AVGO (Broadcom Inc.) — Technical Analysis Report¶

As of 2026-06-30 | Last Trading Day: 2026-06-29 | Last Close: $372.45¶

Executive Summary¶

AVGO experienced a dramatic parabolic surge to a high of $494.22 on June 3, 2026, followed by a violent reversal that erased approximately 25% of the stock's value in under four weeks. The last close of $372.45 on June 29 sits well below both the 10 EMA ($382.64) and the 50 SMA ($410.87), confirming a short- and medium-term bearish trend. Momentum indicators are deeply negative, volume confirms the sell-off, and no exhaustion reversal signal has completed. The prudent stance is defensive.

1. Price Action Overview¶

The Parabolic Spike (May 29 – June 3)¶

  • May 29: Stock closed at $446.06 after a steady multi-week rally from ~$312 in early April.
  • June 1–3: Explosive surge — close went from $459.24 → $480.81 → $478.47, with an intraday high of $494.22 on June 3. Volume surged to 50.1M shares (vs. typical ~20M), confirming blow-off-top dynamics.
  • RSI peaked at 74.75 on June 2, firmly in overbought territory.

The Crash (June 4 – June 5)¶

  • June 4: Gap-down open at $408.34 (from prior close of $478.47), closing at $418.25 on 80.9M volume — the highest volume bar in the entire dataset. This is a classic distribution day.
  • June 5: Further decline to $385.12 on 51.1M volume. Two-day loss: ~19% from the June 3 close.

Choppy Decline (June 8 – June 29)¶

  • Brief bounce attempts on June 8–9 ($395.97 → $391.54) failed to reclaim the 10 EMA.
  • June 10–12: Lower lows, price fell to $371.51.
  • June 15–18: Failed recovery attempt — price spiked to $410.70 on June 18 (on 45M volume) but could not hold, closing the week back at $392.13.
  • June 22: Ex-dividend date ($0.65 dividend); close $392.13.
  • June 23–29: Relentless decline — $380.15 → $382.07 → $378.91 → $365.02 → $372.45. The June 29 intraday low of $360.46 marks the lowest point since the crash.
  • OBV declined from 2,154,965,200 (June 2 peak) to 1,964,553,600 (June 29), a roughly 9% drop in cumulative volume-weighted buying pressure, confirming the selloff is volume-validated.

2. Indicator-by-Indicator Analysis¶

📉 10 EMA — Short-Term Trend: BEARISH¶

Date 10 EMA Close Position
June 3 $447.08 $478.47 Above (bullish)
June 5 $431.53 $385.12 Below (bearish)
June 18 $397.81 $410.70 Briefly above
June 29 $382.64 $372.45 Below by $10.19

The 10 EMA has been declining steadily since June 4. Price has been below the 10 EMA for 17 of the last 19 trading sessions. The brief reclaim on June 18 ($410.70 close vs. $397.81 EMA) was a failed breakout — the next session saw price gap back below. The 10 EMA at $382.64 now serves as immediate overhead resistance.

📉 50 SMA — Medium-Term Trend: BEARISH (Breakdown Confirmed)¶

Date 50 SMA Close Position
June 1 $387.87 $459.24 Above
June 12 $405.81 $381.47 Below
June 18 $411.02 $410.70 At/Below
June 29 $410.87 $372.45 Below by $38.42

Price broke below the 50 SMA around June 10–12 and has not reclaimed it since. The 50 SMA is still rising slightly (from $387.87 to $410.87 over the month) because it lags, but the gap between price and the SMA is widening, not narrowing. The 50 SMA at ~$411 is now major overhead resistance.

📉 MACD — Momentum: BEARISH, Widening¶

Date MACD Value Interpretation

June 9 +2.16 Rapidly fading

June 29 −10.44 Deepest negative reading, still declining

The MACD trajectory tells a clear story: momentum peaked on June 3, collapsed over the next week, crossed below zero on June 10, attempted a recovery around June 18 (contracting to −6.28), but then resumed its decline to −10.44 — a new low. The bearish momentum is accelerating, not stabilizing. No bullish crossover signal is anywhere close.

⚠️ RSI — Momentum: WEAK, Not Yet Oversold¶

Date RSI Zone
June 2 74.75 Overbought (>70)
June 5 39.94 Neutral-bearish
June 10 37.82 Near oversold
June 18 51.33 Brief neutral (bounce)
June 29 42.57 Neutral-bearish

RSI crashed from 74.75 (overbought) to 37.82 in just 6 trading days — an extraordinarily rapid momentum shift. Since then, RSI has oscillated in the 37–51 range without reaching the 30 oversold threshold. This means: - The stock is weak but not yet at an extreme that typically precedes a bounce. - The brief RSI pop to 51.33 on June 18 coincided with the failed price recovery — confirming it was a counter-trend bounce, not a reversal. - No bullish divergence is present: both price and RSI made lower lows from June 18 to June 29.

📉 ADX — Trend Strength: MODERATE, Rebuilding¶

Date ADX Interpretation
June 3 42.63 Very strong trend (upward blow-off)
June 5 25.14 Trend weakening
June 12 19.52 Below 20 (range-bound)
June 22 10.33 No trend (choppy)
June 29 21.17 Just above 20, trend re-emerging

ADX tells a fascinating story: trend strength was extreme during the parabolic blow-off (42.63), then collapsed as the stock entered a choppy decline (10.33 on June 22). Now at 21.17, ADX is just crossing above 20, suggesting a new directional trend may be forming. Given that price is declining, this likely reflects a re-emerging downtrend rather than a reversal. The trend is not yet strong enough (needs >25) for high-confidence trend-following signals.

📉 Bollinger Lower Band — Volatility: EXPANDING, Not Yet Tagged¶

Date Lower Band Close Distance
June 1 $399.94 $459.24 $59.30 above
June 5 $382.62 $385.12 $2.50 above
June 10 $366.71 $371.51 $4.80 above
June 18 $348.75 $410.70 $61.95 above
June 29 $331.14 $372.45 $41.31 above

The Bollinger Lower Band has collapsed from $399.94 to $331.14 — a $68.80 expansion in the lower band, reflecting massive volatility expansion. Critically, the current price of $372.45 is $41.31 above the lower band. The stock came closest to tagging the lower band on June 5 ($385.12 vs. $382.62 band — just $2.50 above), but the band has since fallen faster than the price. This means: - The stock is not statistically oversold by Bollinger standards. - There is room for further downside before hitting the extreme. - The lower band at ~$331 is a key downside target if the downtrend continues.

📉 OBV — Volume Confirmation: BEARISH, No Divergence¶

OBV peaked on June 2 (one day before the price peak), confirming smart-money distribution began at the top. The OBV has declined ~9% from peak and is making lower highs (2,028M on June 18 vs. 2,155M on June 2) and lower lows (1,934M → potentially heading lower). The OBV trend mirrors the price trend — no bullish divergence exists. This means the selling is broad-based and volume-supported, not a low-volume drift.

⏳ TD-9 Sequential — Exhaustion: APPROACHING, Not Complete¶

Timeframe Count Signal
Weekly (Tier 1) +5 (buy-setup) 5 of 9 — approaching exhaustion
Monthly (Tier 2) −3 (sell-setup) 3 of 9 — early stage
Daily (Tier 3) +4 (buy-setup) 4 of 9 — building

The TD-9 provides the most nuanced signal in this analysis: - The weekly buy-setup at count 5 is the most significant. This means the downtrend has been persistent enough to trigger a DeMark buy countdown, but it is only 55% of the way to the 9-count exhaustion signal. We are potentially 4 more weeks from a completed buy signal. - The daily buy-setup at count 4 suggests near-term selling pressure is building toward exhaustion, but again, not there yet. - The monthly sell-setup at −3 is early-stage and secondary, but it indicates that on the highest timeframe, the prior uptrend may still be in a correction phase rather than a full trend reversal.

Key takeaway: The downtrend is approaching exhaustion but has NOT completed. A TD-9 buy signal (count reaching 9) on the weekly timeframe would be a high-confidence reversal trigger — but we are not there yet.

Scenarios¶

Bearish (Most Likely — 60% probability): The downtrend continues. Price tests $360 support, breaks it, and heads toward the Bollinger Lower Band at ~$331. MACD continues to widen. RSI drops below 30 (true oversold). TD-9 daily count climbs toward 9. This scenario would set up a more compelling buy signal in 2–3 weeks.

Neutral/Sideways (25% probability): Price stabilizes in the $360–$390 range as selling pressure exhausts temporarily. ADX stays below 25. TD-9 counts continue building. This would be a consolidation before the next directional move. Wait for a breakout from the range.

Bullish Reversal (15% probability): A catalyst (earnings, sector news, macro) triggers a sharp bounce. Price reclaims the 10 EMA ($382.64) on strong volume, then challenges the 50 SMA ($410.87). MACD begins contracting. This scenario requires confirmation — do not front-run it.

Actionable Recommendations¶

  1. If holding AVGO: The weight of evidence supports reducing or exiting. All indicators are bearish, momentum is accelerating lower, and no reversal signal has triggered. The risk of further decline toward $331 (Bollinger Lower Band) is material. Consider selling into any bounce toward the 10 EMA (~$383).

📊 Comprehensive News & Macro Analysis Report: Broadcom Inc. (AVGO)¶

Analysis Date: June 30, 2026 | Coverage Period: June 23–30, 2026¶

1. AVGO-Specific News Summary¶

🔴 Bearish Signals¶

Co-Founder Selling Pressure & Worst Month in 7 Years Broadcom stock is heading for its worst month in 7 years, a striking divergence from the broader semiconductor rally. Notably, Broadcom's co-founder has sold over $720M in quarterly stake sales, signaling possible insider caution. Insider selling at this magnitude—especially during a broad-based chip stock rally—warrants close attention. While insider sales can reflect routine diversification, the combination with AVGO's sharp monthly underperformance is a red flag.

Stock Underperformance vs. Semiconductor Peers While the broader semiconductor sector has surged—climbing to a record 19.7% of the S&P 500 as the AI rally reshapes the index—AVGO appears to be lagging. Intel and AMD both jumped 7% as chip stocks caught a risk-on bid, yet AVGO's negative monthly performance suggests sector-specific headwinds or company-specific concerns are weighing on the stock.

🟢 Bullish Signals¶

Jalapeño AI Accelerator Partnership with OpenAI Broadcom's "Jalapeño" AI accelerator partnership with OpenAI represents a significant strategic expansion into custom AI silicon. This positions AVGO as a key partner for one of the most prominent AI companies, potentially opening a large recurring revenue stream as OpenAI scales its infrastructure.

JPMorgan Defends TPU v9 Program JPMorgan has pushed back on delay fears surrounding AVGO's TPU v9 program, asserting it is firmly on schedule. This is a critical positive, as the TPU partnership with Google is one of AVGO's most important growth drivers. Market concerns about delays were apparently overblown, and JPM's endorsement should help stabilize sentiment.

Tender Offer Execution — Balance Sheet Management AVGO raised its tender-offer cap to $3 billion and accepted $2.9 billion in notes, demonstrating proactive debt management. This is a sign of financial discipline and could help reduce interest expenses, particularly relevant in a higher-for-longer rate environment.

New Chip Could Be a Game Changer A new chip development (details emerging) is being flagged as a potential game changer for Broadcom stock, suggesting the company's product pipeline remains robust.

⚖️ Neutral / Context¶

Semiconductor Sector at Record S&P 500 Weight Semiconductors now represent 19.7% of the S&P 500, a record high driven by the AI rally. While this validates the long-term thesis for AVGO, it also raises concentration risk concerns—any broad AI de-rating could disproportionately impact semiconductor names.

2. Global Macroeconomic Environment¶

🏛️ Federal Reserve: Leadership Change Looms¶

Kevin Warsh Taking Over the Fed A major macro event is unfolding: Kevin Warsh is taking over the Federal Reserve, and his first meeting could "slam the stock market." Warsh is perceived as potentially more hawkish, and markets are anxious about the policy direction under his leadership.

Inflation Target Concerns There is growing debate about whether the Fed can allow 4% to become the new 2% inflation target. If inflation remains sticky, a Warsh-led Fed could maintain a hawkish stance longer than markets currently expect, which would be negative for rate-sensitive growth stocks like AVGO.

🛢️ Geopolitics & Commodities¶

Iran Conflict & Oil Price Collapse - The Iran war is adding fuel to inflation concerns, with ripple effects on consumer goods (e.g., shoe prices rising). - However, oil prices are set for their steepest monthly and quarterly loss since 2020, which is paradoxically disinflationary for energy but reflects demand concerns and supply dynamics. - Stock bulls appear in control of the quarter as oil tumbles, with gold and yen also falling.

Copper Supply Crunch A global copper supply crunch is intensifying, impacting the auto industry (Tesla stock fell partly due to copper cost concerns). For AVGO, copper is a key input for semiconductor manufacturing, and rising copper costs could pressure margins.

📈 Market Valuation Concerns¶

Dangerous Valuation Trap Barron's warns that stocks are flirting with a dangerous valuation trap, with the market at elevated multiples. For high-PE semiconductor stocks like AVGO, this is particularly relevant—any earnings disappointment could trigger sharp corrections.

Tech Slump Risk This week's confluence of events—tech slump, Iran strikes, inflation data, and SpaceX developments—was flagged as potentially market-making or breaking. The Nasdaq showed strength with a tech player hitting new highs, suggesting selective rotation rather than broad-based selling.

🤖 AI & IPO Pipeline¶

$4 Trillion IPO Question OpenAI, Anthropic, and SpaceX IPOs could collectively raise up to $4 trillion, representing a massive capital raise that could divert liquidity from existing AI-exposed stocks. If these IPOs proceed, they could create selling pressure on established semiconductor names like AVGO as investors rotate into new AI pure-plays.

Quantum Computing Emerging D-Wave Quantum is "winning over Washington," and quantum computing stocks are gaining traction. While not an immediate threat to AVGO, this represents a longer-term technology shift that investors should monitor.

3. Consumer & Retail Signals¶

  • Job concerns and price hikes suggest a shaky consumer, which could slow overall economic activity.
  • Walmart's AI tool (Sparky) usage doubled, suggesting AI adoption is broadening—positive for the secular AI thesis that underpins AVGO's growth.
  • Ross Stores and Target showing resilience, but broader consumer caution signals warrant monitoring.

  2. Warsh Fed takeover could introduce hawkish surprise, pressuring high-PE growth stocks
  3. Valuation trap risk in a market at elevated multiples

  2. OpenAI Jalapeño partnership opens new revenue avenues

  4. Semiconductor sector at record S&P weight validates the long-term thesis
  5. New chip development could be a game changer

Net Assessment:¶

AVGO faces a tension between strong fundamental catalysts (TPU v9 on schedule, OpenAI partnership, new chips, balance sheet management) and significant near-term headwinds (worst month in 7 years, massive insider selling, hawkish Fed transition, valuation risks, copper cost pressures). 1. Company Profile & Overview¶

Broadcom Inc. is a global technology leader in the design, development, and supply of a broad range of semiconductor and infrastructure software solutions. The company operates through two primary segments: Semiconductor Solutions and Infrastructure Software. Broadcom's portfolio spans networking chips, broadband, wireless connectivity, storage adapters, and cybersecurity/infrastructure software (significantly expanded via the VMware acquisition completed in late FY2024).

  • Beta: 1.433 (higher than market volatility)

  • Deleveraging in Progress: Net debt has declined from $57.80B (Apr 2025) to $45.28B (Apr 2026) — a $12.5B reduction in one year. The company is actively using its massive cash flow to pay down acquisition debt.
  • Total Debt: Relatively stable at $64-67B range over the past year. Long-term debt stands at $62.66B, with current debt at $2.25B.
  • Current Ratio: 2.24 — strong short-term liquidity, up significantly from 1.08 (Apr 2025) as current assets grew faster than current liabilities.
  • Cash Position: $19.63B, up from $9.47B a year ago — a $10.2B increase, reflecting strong cash generation and disciplined capital allocation.
  • Debt to Equity: 74.02 — elevated due to VMware acquisition financing but improving as equity grows and debt declines.
  • Receivables Surge: Accounts receivable grew from $5.56B (Apr 2025) to $10.83B (Apr 2026), up 94.6%, tracking closely with revenue growth. The allowance for doubtful accounts remains minimal.

Critical Cash Flow Insights:

  1. FCF Growth Trajectory: Free cash flow grew from $16.31B (FY2022) → $17.63B (FY2023) → $19.41B (FY2024) → $26.91B (FY2025). This represents a 65% increase over three years. The TTM FCF is approximately $27.21B, and the quarterly trend shows continued acceleration ($10.26B in Q2 FY2026 alone).

  2. Extremely Capital-Light Model: CapEx of just $0.62B against $63.89B revenue (FY2025) yields a CapEx/revenue ratio of less than 1%. This is a hallmark of a fab-less semiconductor and software business model — extremely high FCF conversion.

  3. FCF Conversion: FY2025 FCF/EBITDA conversion = $26.91B / $34.71B = 77.6% — outstanding cash conversion.

  4. Dividend Growth: Annual dividends paid grew from $7.03B (FY2022) to $11.14B (FY2025), a 58.5% increase. The current dividend yield of 0.70% is modest, but the absolute dollar amount and growth rate are significant.

  5. Share Repurchases: The company repurchased $6.31B in FY2025 (after $12.39B in FY2024). In Q1 FY2026, buybacks spiked to $7.85B, suggesting management sees value at current levels. Share count has been relatively stable at ~4.76B shares.

  6. Stock-Based Compensation: SBC was $7.57B in FY2025 (up from $5.74B in FY2024 and $2.17B in FY2023). This is a significant non-cash expense that dilutes shareholders and should be factored into valuation. SBC as a % of revenue was ~11.9% in FY2025.

  7. Debt Repayment: The company repaid $18.48B in long-term debt in FY2025 while issuing $15.67B, for a net repayment of $2.81B. This active debt management, combined with strong cash generation, is steadily improving the balance sheet.

  8. Interest Costs: Annual interest expense was $3.21B in FY2025 (up from $1.62B in FY2023), reflecting debt taken on for the VMware acquisition. Quarterly interest expense has stabilized at ~$776-801M. The interest coverage ratio (EBIT/Interest Expense) = $25.94B / $3.21B = ~8.1x — very comfortable.

7. Growth Trajectory & Forward Outlook¶

Revenue Growth¶

  • FY2022 → FY2025 CAGR: 24.2% (including VMware acquisition impact)
  • Organic growth (excluding VMware) is estimated in the 10-15% range, driven by AI networking chips (Tomahawk, Jericho), custom AI accelerators (XPUs), and broadband.
  • Quarterly revenue acceleration: Q2 FY2026 revenue of $22.19B represents the highest quarterly revenue ever, with QoQ growth of 14.9% — an unusual acceleration for a company of this size.

Earnings Growth¶

  • Diluted EPS: FY2022: $2.65 → FY2025: $4.77 (FY2024 was depressed by acquisition charges at $1.23)
  • Forward EPS of $19.40 vs. TTM EPS of $6.00 implies the market expects earnings to more than triple. This likely reflects:
  • Full VMware synergy realization
  • Continued AI semiconductor ramp
  • Reduced restructuring/acquisition charges
  • Potential tax benefits

AI Catalyst¶

Broadcom is a major beneficiary of AI infrastructure buildout through: - Custom AI accelerators (XPUs) for hyperscale customers (Google, Meta, etc.) - High-speed networking silicon (Tomahawk 5, Jericho3-AI) critical for AI cluster connectivity - VMware Private AI and virtualization software for enterprise AI deployments

8. Risk Factors¶

  1. High Goodwill/Intangibles: $126.1B in goodwill + intangibles (70% of total assets). Any impairment could materially impact the balance sheet.

  2. Elevated Debt: $64.9B total debt, though declining. Interest costs of ~$3.2B/year are a drag on earnings.

  3. Customer Concentration: Broadcom's top customers (especially in AI/custom silicon) represent a significant portion of revenue. Loss of a major design win could be material.

  4. Stock-Based Compensation: $7.57B annually represents real shareholder dilution. Adjusted (non-GAAP) figures that exclude SBC overstate true earnings power.

  5. Acquisition Integration Risk: While VMware integration appears on track, large acquisitions carry execution risk and cultural integration challenges.

  6. Cyclicality: Semiconductor demand is inherently cyclical. A downturn in enterprise IT spending or AI infrastructure investment could impact growth.

  7. Geopolitical Risk: Semiconductor supply chains are exposed to U.S.-China trade tensions, Taiwan supply dependencies, and export controls.

  8. Valuation Risk: TTM P/E of 62.5x is high. While the forward P/E of 19.3x and PEG of 0.66x suggest growth justifies the premium, any miss on forward earnings expectations could trigger a sharp correction.

9. Capital Allocation Strategy¶

Broadcom employs a disciplined capital allocation framework: 1. Debt repayment — Priority post-VMware acquisition (net debt down $12.5B in one year) 2. Dividends — Growing steadily, $11.14B in FY2025 3. Share repurchases — Opportunistic, $6.31B in FY2025, $7.85B in Q1 FY2026 alone 4. R&D investment — $10.98B in FY2025, up 110% from FY2023 5. Strategic M&A — Track record of value-accretive acquisitions (though likely paused near-term given VMware integration)

11. Key Takeaways for Traders¶

  1. Revenue Acceleration: Broadcom is experiencing its strongest sequential and year-over-year revenue growth in recent history, driven by AI demand and VMware synergies.

  2. Operating Leverage: Net income is growing nearly 2x faster than revenue (87% vs. 48%), demonstrating powerful operating leverage as fixed costs are spread over a larger revenue base.

  3. FCF Powerhouse: $27B+ in TTM free cash flow with <1% CapEx/revenue ratio gives Broadcom enormous financial flexibility for debt reduction, dividends, buybacks, and R&D.

  4. Deleveraging Story: Net debt has declined $12.5B in one year. At current FCF generation rates, Broadcom could eliminate net debt within ~2 years if it prioritized debt repayment.

  5. Valuation Gap: The discrepancy between TTM P/E (62.5x) and Forward P/E (19.3x) is extreme. This suggests either (a) the market expects massive earnings growth, or (b) TTM earnings are temporarily depressed by one-time charges. Traders should verify the components of the Forward EPS estimate.

  6. PEG Ratio Attractive: At 0.66, the PEG ratio suggests the stock is undervalued relative to its growth rate — a potential buy signal for growth-oriented investors.

  7. Momentum: Trading above both 50-day and 200-day moving averages, with the 50-day above the 200-day (golden cross territory). The stock is up significantly from its 52-week low of $262.66.

  8. Catalyst Calendar: Traders should monitor upcoming quarterly earnings (expected around September 2026 for Q3 FY2026), AI-related product announcements, and any updates on VMware integration milestones.

Final Assessment¶

Broadcom Inc. (AVGO) presents a compelling fundamental profile characterized by accelerating revenue growth, industry-leading margins, massive free cash flow generation, and active deleveraging. The VMware acquisition is clearly delivering synergies, while AI-related semiconductor demand provides a powerful organic growth catalyst. The PEG ratio of 0.66 and forward P/E of 19.3x suggest the stock may still offer value despite its high trailing P/E. Key risks include elevated debt levels (though declining), significant stock-based compensation, negative tangible book value, and concentration in AI/custom silicon customer relationships.

For traders: The combination of accelerating quarterly revenue, expanding margins, and strong cash flow momentum supports a constructive near-to-medium-term outlook. However, the elevated TTM P/E means the stock is priced for continued execution — any earnings miss or AI demand slowdown could trigger significant volatility given the 1.43 beta.

FINAL TRANSACTION PROPOSAL: BUY

II. Research Team Decision¶

Bull Researcher¶

Bull Analyst: # 🐂 BULL OPENING ARGUMENT: Broadcom Inc. (AVGO) — The AI Infrastructure Juggernaut at a Discount

Opening Statement¶

Let me be direct: the bear case for AVGO is overwhelmingly focused on short-term technical damage and one headline-grabbing insider sale, while completely missing the fundamental transformation happening beneath the surface. What we're looking at is a company that just grew revenue 47.9% year-over-year, expanded operating margins to nearly 49%, generated $10.26 billion in free cash flow in a single quarter, and is positioning itself as the non-Nvidia AI infrastructure play with confirmed partnerships with both Google and OpenAI. The stock has been punished for a parabolic spike that was never sustainable — but the underlying business has never been stronger.

Let me walk through why this selloff is a generational buying opportunity, not the start of a structural decline.

1. The Fundamental Growth Story is Accelerating, Not Decelerating¶

The technical report paints a picture of a stock in freefall. But let's look at what the business is actually doing:

Metric Q2 FY25 (Apr '25) Q2 FY26 (Apr '26) YoY Growth
Revenue $15.00B $22.19B +47.9%
Operating Income $5.92B $10.86B +83.5%
Net Income $4.97B $9.31B +87.4%
Free Cash Flow $6.41B $10.26B +60.1%

Net income is growing nearly 2x faster than revenue — this is textbook operating leverage. The VMware integration is delivering synergies faster than expected, and AI semiconductor demand is accelerating. Q2 FY26 was the highest quarterly revenue in company history, with sequential growth of 14.9% — unusual acceleration for a company already at a $90B+ annualized revenue run rate.

And the forward guidance? Q3 AI chip growth is guided at over 200% year-over-year. This isn't a company losing momentum; it's a company in the early innings of a massive AI infrastructure buildout cycle.

2. The Valuation Case: PEG of 0.66 Screams "Undervalued"¶

The bear case loves to cite the trailing P/E of 62.5x. But that metric is artificially inflated by one-time VMware acquisition charges that depressed trailing earnings. Here's what matters:

  • Forward P/E: 19.34x — for a company growing revenue at 48% and earnings at 87%, this is exceptionally attractive
  • PEG Ratio: 0.66 — anything below 1.0 signals the stock is undervalued relative to its growth rate
  • Forward EPS: $19.40 vs. TTM EPS of $6.00 — the market expects earnings to more than triple as acquisition charges roll off and AI revenue ramps

To put this in perspective: NVDA trades at a forward P/E of ~35x with comparable growth rates. AVGO offers similar AI exposure at nearly half the forward multiple. The median Street price target is $525 — representing ~41% upside from the current $372.45.

3. The AI Catalyst Stack is Unmatched Outside of Nvidia¶

The bear report completely ignores the most important development: Broadcom is building a diversified AI infrastructure franchise that doesn't rely on a single product or customer.

Three Pillars of AI Revenue:

  1. Google TPU v9 Program — JPMorgan has explicitly confirmed this is "firmly on schedule." This was a key overhang that has now been removed. The TPU partnership with Google is one of the largest custom silicon programs in the world.

  2. OpenAI "Jalapeño" AI Accelerator Partnership — A new custom AI chip developed in collaboration with OpenAI. This positions AVGO as a key enabler of next-gen AI infrastructure beyond Nvidia's orbit. When OpenAI — the company driving the AI revolution — chooses Broadcom as a silicon partner, that's a seismic validation.

  3. High-Speed Networking Silicon — Tomahawk 5 and Jericho3-AI are critical for AI cluster connectivity. Every AI cluster needs networking chips, and Broadcom is the dominant player. You can't build an AI datacenter without Broadcom's networking silicon.

This is the bull case in a single sentence: Nvidia makes the brains, but Broadcom makes the nervous system.

4. Countering the Bear's Key Arguments¶

Bear Argument #1: "All 8 technical indicators are bearish"¶

My response: Technical indicators are backward-looking by definition. They tell you what happened, not what will happen. The stock surged 58% in two months (from $312 to $494) — of course the technicals broke down after a blow-off top. That's not a thesis-changing event; that's profit-taking after an overextended rally.

More importantly, the fundamentals report shows the stock is still trading above its 200-day moving average ($361.33) — the long-term trend remains intact. The June 29 intraday low of $360.46 tested this level and held. The 200 DMA is the line that matters for trend determination, and it held.

The TD-9 weekly buy-setup is at 5/9 and building — the bear's own report acknowledges this is the most forward-looking signal, and it's approaching exhaustion. Smart money is accumulating.

Bear Argument #2: "Co-founder sold $720M — insider signal"¶

My response: Let me put this in context. The co-founder's stake is worth billions. $720M in a single quarter sounds dramatic, but for a trillion-dollar company founder, this is routine diversification and estate planning. The critical question is: did the selling accelerate during the crash, or was it pre-planned under a 10b5-1 plan?

The vast majority of executive sales at this scale are executed under pre-arranged trading plans that were set up months in advance. The co-founder didn't wake up on June 3 and decide to dump shares — that plan was likely established when the stock was at $312.

Meanwhile, the company itself repurchased $7.85 billion in stock in Q1 FY2026 alone. If the co-founder's sale signals caution, then the company's $7.85B buyback signals the opposite. Who knows the business better — the co-founder diversifying, or the board authorizing $7.85B in buybacks? I'll take the board's signal.

Bear Argument #3: "Worst month in 7 years"¶

My response: The worst month in 7 years followed the best multi-month rally in years. The stock went from $312 to $494 in two months — a 58% gain that was never sustainable. What we're seeing is mean reversion after a parabolic spike, not a fundamental deterioration.

The sentiment report confirms this is part of a June rotation across Big Tech, not an AVGO-specific problem. As @BreakoutLife noted on StockTwits: "June isn't a normal sell-off — it's rotation." The semiconductor sector still represents a record 19.7% of the S&P 500 — the structural tailwind is intact.

Bear Argument #4: "Hawkish Fed under Warsh"¶

My response: AVGO is not a rate-sensitive stock in the traditional sense. It generates $27 billion in annual free cash flow with a capital-light model (CapEx/revenue < 1%). The company doesn't need cheap debt to grow — it funds its own growth. The tender offer to retire $2.9B in notes demonstrates proactive debt management regardless of the rate environment.

Furthermore, if the Warsh Fed tightens and causes a broader market selloff, high-quality cash-flow generators like AVGO become flight-to-safety destinations. Investors fleeing speculative AI names will rotate into AVGO, which has real revenue, real earnings, and real cash flow.

Bear Argument #5: "Copper costs could pressure margins"¶

My response: Broadcom is a fabless semiconductor company — it designs chips but doesn't manufacture them. Fabrication is done by TSMC and other foundries. Copper cost pressures would affect the foundries, not Broadcom's design margins. And with gross margins at 76.3% and operating margins at 49%, there's enormous cushion for any input cost pass-through.

5. The Balance Sheet is Strengthening, Not Weakening¶

1. The 200% AI Growth Challenge — Answered¶

The bear demands I disclose the "absolute revenue base" for the 200% AI chip growth guidance, implying it's growing off a tiny number. Let me use the data we do have to triangulate:

This is textbook sector rotation within a sector rotation:

  1. Phase 1 (April-May): Capital concentrated in AVGO (the AI infrastructure leader) → +58%
  2. Phase 2 (June): Capital rotated from AVGO into semiconductor laggards (INTC, AMD) → +7% each

The bear is comparing AVGO's post-surge correction to INTC/AMD's catch-up bounce and calling it "company-specific underperformance." That's like saying the marathon leader is "underperforming" because the runners in 20th place are sprinting to catch up.

The bear calls $19.40 forward EPS a "fantasy" requiring five simultaneous assumptions. Let me build it bottom-up with actual data:

FY2025 Actuals (from the fundamental report): - Revenue: $63.89B - Operating Income: $26.07B (40.8% margin) - Net Income: $23.13B - Interest Expense: $3.21B - Tax Provision: -$397M (benefit)

Adjusted FY2026 EPS estimate: - GAAP Net Income: ~$32.6B - + SBC: $7.57B - + Amortization: $2.5B - + Restructuring: $1.5B - Adjusted Net Income: ~$44.2B - Adjusted EPS: ~$9.28

Here's what matters: Even at my conservative $9.28 adjusted EPS, the forward P/E drops to $372.45 / $9.28 = ~40x — still high, but declining rapidly as earnings grow. And at $6.85 GAAP EPS, the P/E is ~54x — below the current 62.5x. The earnings trajectory is unmistakably upward, regardless of which EPS measure you use.

The bear's error isn't in questioning the $19.40 — it's in using that skepticism to dismiss the direction of earnings growth. Earnings are growing powerfully. The exact destination is debatable; the trajectory is not.

5. The PEG Ratio: Even on "Honest" Numbers, It's Attractive¶

The bear recalculates PEG using 10-15% organic growth and gets 1.5-2.2. But I've just shown that semiconductor segment growth is ~50% organically, driven by AI. The 10-15% figure the bear uses is simply wrong for the current period.

Let's use a blended, conservative approach: - Semiconductor segment (60% of revenue): 40% growth (below the actual ~50%) - Software segment (40% of revenue): 15% growth (VMware synergies) - Blended growth: ~30%

At 30% growth and a forward P/E of 40x (using my conservative adjusted EPS): PEG = 1.33

That's above 1.0, but not dramatically — and it's based on conservative assumptions. If AI revenue continues accelerating at >200% YoY, the growth rate could easily exceed 30%, pushing PEG back below 1.0.

The bear's PEG of 1.5-2.2 requires using both an artificially low growth rate (10-15%) and the inflated TTM P/E (62.5x). When you use honest numbers on both sides, the valuation is reasonable, not cheap, but reasonable — and for a company with Broadcom's competitive positioning, reasonable is a buying opportunity.

  • The 200 DMA is at $361.33 — but this is a rising average. It was at ~$340 a month ago and ~$320 two months ago. The 200 DMA is accelerating upward, which means support is strengthening, not weakening.

The bear assumes AVGO is speculative — it's not. It has $27B in FCF, 49% operating margins, and a diversified revenue base.

7. The AI Concentration Risk — A Valid Concern, But Overstated¶

The bear raises legitimate points about customer concentration (Google TPU) and competitive threats (Marvell, NVIDIA Spectrum-X). Let me address these honestly:

Google TPU Concentration¶

Yes, Google is a powerful customer. But the bear's "Google could in-source" argument has been circulating for five years — and Google hasn't done it. Why?

  1. Chip design is incredibly hard. Google's core competency is software and systems, not semiconductor design at scale. Broadcom brings decades of IP, design teams, and manufacturing relationships.
  2. The partnership is economically rational for both sides. Google gets world-class custom silicon without the capital expense of building a chip design empire. Broadcom gets guaranteed volume and a strategic partnership. Both parties benefit.
  3. TPU v9 being "on schedule" signals continued commitment. Google doesn't invest in a next-generation program with a partner they plan to abandon.

OpenAI Jalapeño — Not Just a Partnership Announcement¶

The bear dismisses Jalapeño as "future potential revenue." But the news report describes it as a developed chip — "a new custom AI chip developed in collaboration with OpenAI." This isn't an MOU; it's a product. The revenue timeline is likely FY2027, not some distant future.

More importantly, the OpenAI partnership signals that Broadcom is the preferred custom silicon partner for AI companies. When OpenAI — which has its pick of partners — chooses Broadcom, it validates the entire custom AI silicon thesis. This is a strategic moat expansion, not just a revenue line.

Competition (Marvell, NVIDIA Spectrum-X)¶

Marvell is a legitimate competitor in custom silicon — but they're a fraction of Broadcom's size and R&D budget ($10.98B vs Marvell's ~$1.8B). NVIDIA's Spectrum-X is a threat in networking, but Broadcom's Tomahawk/Jericho franchise has years of design wins and customer lock-in. Hyperscalers don't switch networking silicon providers lightly — it requires re-architecting entire datacenter fabrics.

8. The Balance Sheet — The Bear's Selective Framing¶

The bear lists five balance sheet "risk factors" but frames each in the most negative possible light:

Bear's Claim Full Context
"Negative tangible book value of -$38.44B" This is standard for serial acquirers. Microsoft, Cisco, and Oracle all have similar profiles. The market values earnings power, not book value, for asset-light tech companies.
"Goodwill at 54.6% of assets" Goodwill reflects the premium paid for acquired earnings power. As long as acquired businesses perform (VMware is delivering synergies), goodwill is justified. Impairment requires a sustained business decline — not a one-month stock selloff.
"SBC of $7.57B annually" SBC as a % of revenue has been declining — from ~16% in FY2023 to ~11.9% in FY2025. The trend is improving as revenue grows faster than compensation.
"Debt still elevated at $64.9B" Net debt has declined $12.5B in one year. At current FCF generation, the deleveraging trajectory is clear and accelerating.
"Customer concentration" Broadcom has thousands of customers across networking, broadband, wireless, storage, and software. AI custom silicon concentration is a growth concern, not a survival concern.

The bear's balance sheet critique would be valid if Broadcom were struggling to service its debt. But with $27B in annual FCF, 8.1x interest coverage, and a 2.24 current ratio, the balance sheet is strengthening every quarter. The bear is pointing at the starting point and ignoring the trajectory.

10. The Bear's Macro Risks — Measured, Not Dismissed¶

I don't dismiss macro risks — I price them in. Let me address each:

Warsh Fed¶

A hawkish Fed is already widely expected and priced in. The stock has already fallen 25% — how much more multiple compression can occur? The forward P/E is already at 19x (street estimates). If Warsh is less hawkish than feared, AVGO rallies hard on a beta-driven relief bounce.

IPO Liquidity Drain¶

The $4T IPO figure across OpenAI/Anthropic/SpaceX is a multi-year pipeline, not a single event. And these IPOs would actually validate the AI thesis, drawing more capital into the sector. When OpenAI IPOs, its partnership with Broadcom becomes a publicly verifiable relationship — potentially attracting capital to AVGO, not draining it.

Copper Costs¶

The bear is right that TSMC may pass through costs — but Broadcom's 76.3% gross margin provides enormous cushion. A 1-2% increase in foundry costs would reduce gross margin to 74-75% — still elite. And Broadcom's pricing power in AI silicon (where demand vastly exceeds supply) means cost pass-throughs to customers are feasible.

Geopolitical Risk¶

This is a real risk — but it's a sector-wide risk, not AVGO-specific. Every semiconductor company faces China/Taiwan exposure. If anything, Broadcom's diversified manufacturing relationships (TSMC, Samsung Foundry, GlobalFoundries for older nodes) provide more optionality than pure-play NVIDIA.

11. Final Synthesis: Why the Bull Case Wins¶

The bear has built a sophisticated argument, but it rests on three foundational errors:

Error #1: Using Stale Growth Assumptions¶

The bear's "10-15% organic growth" figure is outdated. Q2 FY26 semiconductor revenue grew ~50% YoY — after VMware was fully lapped. The AI ramp is delivering organic growth that rivals or exceeds the acquisition-driven headline number.

Error #2: Treating Technical Damage as Structural¶

The bear treats 19 trading days of price action as more meaningful than 8 quarters of accelerating fundamental performance. The stock surged 58% in two months and gave back 25% — it's still up 19% in three months and 42% year-to-date. This is a correction within a bull trend, not a trend reversal.

Error #3: Demanding Perfection from the Bull While Accepting Mediocrity from the Bear¶

The bear's downside scenario requires: (1) 200 DMA breaking, (2) Bollinger Lower Band tagging, (3) AI narrative cracking, (4) Warsh Fed tightening, (5) IPO drain materializing, and (6) Q3 earnings disappointing. That's six simultaneous negative outcomes. The bull case requires: (1) AI revenue continues ramping as guided, and (2) the stock eventually reflects its earnings power. That's two outcomes, both of which are supported by current data.

The Bottom Line¶

The bear asks you to sell a company that: - Just delivered $22.19B in quarterly revenue (up 47.9% YoY) - Generated $10.26B in free cash flow in a single quarter - Holds confirmed AI partnerships with Google and OpenAI - Is deleveraging rapidly (net debt down $12.5B in one year) - Trades at a forward P/E of 19.3x (street estimates) with AI revenue growing >200% YoY - Is above its 200-day moving average with the long-term trend intact

...because of 19 days of technical damage, a pre-planned insider sale, and macro risks that are already priced in.

The bear calls this a "falling knife with a fundamental narrative attached." I call it a fundamental narrative with a temporary technical discount attached. The knife is decelerating (TD-9 building, 200 DMA holding, RSI not yet oversold), and the fundamental story is getting stronger, not weaker.

When a company growing revenue at 48% and earnings at 87% trades at a forward P/E of 19x, the market is offering you a gift. The bear's advice to "wait for a better entry" assumes you can time the bottom perfectly — but by the time the TD-9 reaches 9 and the Bollinger Band tags, the stock may already be rallying on Q3 earnings catalysts.

My recommendation stands: BUY AVGO at current levels ($372.45), with a stop at $355 (below 200 DMA). The risk is defined and small; the reward is asymmetric and growing.

The bear says "listen to the market, not the story." I say: the market is telling you the story — you just have to know how to read it. The story is in the $22.19B revenue print, the $10.26B free cash flow, the 200% AI growth guidance, and the 200 DMA that held. That's the market speaking. The bear just isn't listening.

Opening: Owning the EPS Discrepancy — and Why It Doesn't Change the Thesis¶

The bear thinks they've landed a knockout blow. They haven't. But let me start with intellectual honesty, because that's how you build a case that survives contact with reality.

Yes, my bottom-up model produced $6.85 GAAP and $9.28 adjusted EPS — not $19.40. The bear calls this a "self-inflicted wound." I call it doing the work. What my model likely underestimated:

  3. Share count reduction: The company repurchased $7.85B in Q1 FY26 alone. At an average price of ~$350, that's roughly 175 million shares retired — reducing the share count from ~4.76B to ~4.58B. My static 4.76B assumption overstated dilution.

  4. Tax rate normalization: FY2025 had a negative tax provision (-$397M benefit). My model assumed an 18% tax rate. But if the company's effective tax rate normalizes lower due to structuring, R&D credits, or international mix, the tax burden could be significantly less.

The bear knows this but uses TTM P/E anyway because it produces a scary number.

  2. Even at my conservative $11.96 adjusted EPS, the forward P/E is $372/$11.96 = ~31x. The bear's claim that 40x is "expensive" ignores that this is a company with 49% operating margins, $27B in FCF, and confirmed partnerships with the two most important AI companies on earth.

2. The Growth Debate: The Bear's "10-15%" Is the Real Fabrication¶

The bear repeatedly cites the fundamental report's statement: "Organic growth (excluding VMware) is estimated in the 10-15% range." They treat this as gospel. Let me examine what this statement actually means — and doesn't mean.

What the Report Actually Says¶

The full quote from the fundamental report reads:

"Organic growth (excluding VMware) is estimated in the 10-15% range, driven by AI networking chips (Tomahawk, Jericho), custom AI accelerators (XPUs), and broadband."

This is a historical characterization of organic growth — it describes the trajectory before the AI ramp fully materialized. The report was compiled using FY2022-FY2025 annual data, a period when AI was still in early adoption.

Here's what the bear ignores: Q2 FY26 (ended April 2026) is the first quarter where AI infrastructure spending has fully materialized in financial results. The >200% YoY AI chip growth guidance isn't a historical trend — it's a forward data point that postdates the 10-15% estimate.

The Math the Bear Won't Do¶

The bear says my ~50% semiconductor growth estimate is "fabricated" because the report doesn't provide segment-level Q2 FY26 data. Fine — let's use only data that IS in the report:

Data Point (from report) Value
Q2 FY26 Total Revenue $22.19B

YoY Growth +47.9%
VMware Acquisition Close November 2023

The anniversary effect (comparing 18 months vs. 30 months of VMware) adds roughly 6 months of incremental VMware revenue to the YoY comparison. If VMware contributes ~$13B annually (based on its pre-acquisition run rate), 6 months of incremental revenue is ~$6.5B.

Organic YoY growth calculation: - Total YoY growth: $22.19B - $15.00B = $7.19B - VMware anniversary effect: ~$6.5B (incremental 6 months) - Organic growth: $7.19B - $6.5B = $0.69B - Organic growth rate: $0.69B / $15.00B = ~4.6%

Answer: because my assumption about VMware's contribution is wrong. VMware was already fully lapped by Q2 FY25 (18 months on the books). The "anniversary effect" I calculated above doesn't apply — VMware revenue was already in the Q2 FY25 base. The incremental VMware contribution from Q2 FY25 to Q2 FY26 is just normal software growth (15-20% from cross-selling synergies), not a step-function addition.

If VMware grew at 18% (reasonable for mature enterprise software with synergy upside) and total grew at 48%, then the semiconductor segment — which represents the majority of revenue — must be growing at a rate substantially above 48% to pull the average up. Basic algebra requires semiconductor growth of 60%+ if software grows at 18%.

The bear's "10-15% organic growth" figure from the fundamental report describes a past era. The Q2 FY26 results — generated entirely within the provided data — are mathematically inconsistent with 10-15% organic growth unless VMware grew at an impossible rate. The AI ramp has fundamentally changed Broadcom's growth trajectory, and the data proves it.

3. The Concentration Risk: Reframed as Competitive Moat¶

The bear raises valid concerns about customer concentration. But they frame it as pure risk; I frame it as competitive validation. Let me address each:

Google TPU: Monopsony Power Cuts Both Ways¶

The bear says Google has "monopsony power" and can squeeze Broadcom's margins. But consider:

  1. Broadcom's Q2 FY26 operating margin expanded to 48.9% from 38.1% a year ago. If Google were squeezing margins, we'd see the opposite — margin compression, not expansion. The data directly contradicts the bear's thesis.

  2. The TPU partnership is a co-development relationship, not a vendor-buyer transaction. Broadcom isn't selling commoditized chips to Google — they're jointly designing custom silicon that neither company could build alone. This creates bilateral dependency, not monopsony.

  3. Switching costs are enormous. A custom silicon program like TPU involves years of co-design, testing, and optimization. Google couldn't switch to Marvell without delaying their AI roadmap by 2-3 years. The switching cost is the moat.

OpenAI Jalapeño: The Strategic Value Exceeds the Revenue Timeline¶

The bear emphasizes that Jalapeño revenue is "likely FY2027" — 6-12 months away. This is a fair point about timing. But the bear misses the strategic significance:

  1. OpenAI chose Broadcom over NVIDIA for custom silicon. This is a profound signal. The company that knows AI better than anyone chose the company that knows custom silicon better than anyone. That validation attracts other AI companies to Broadcom's custom silicon platform.

  2. The custom silicon TAM is expanding exponentially. Every hyperscaler and AI lab is realizing that GPUs are too expensive and power-hungry for inference at scale. Custom accelerators (XPUs) are the future. Broadcom is positioned as the default partner for this transition.

  3. Revenue timing is a feature, not a bug. The bear says "buy now based on revenue that won't materialize for two quarters." I say: that's exactly when you should buy — before the revenue materializes and the stock reprices. By FY2027, when Jalapeño revenue hits, the stock will be at $525+, not $372.

Competitive Threats: Marvell and NVIDIA Spectrum-X¶

The bear compares Broadcom's networking dominance to Cisco's router monopoly — suggesting disruption is inevitable. This analogy is flawed:

  • Cisco was disrupted by software (SDN) and commoditized hardware (white-box switching). Broadcom's networking chips are the silicon inside those white-box switches. Broadcom benefited from Cisco's disruption, they didn't suffer from it.
  • Marvell winning "one or two design wins" doesn't threaten Broadcom's trajectory. The AI networking market is growing so fast that there's room for multiple winners. Broadcom's installed base and design pipeline provide years of runway.
  • NVIDIA Spectrum-X is a real threat — but to Cisco and Arista, not to Broadcom. Broadcom makes the chips that go into switches; NVIDIA's Spectrum-X is a switch system. They're at different levels of the stack.

5. The SBC Argument: The Bear's Circular Logic Claim Is Itself Circular¶

The bear says my SBC defense is "circular" — if SBC is priced in, use GAAP; if it's non-cash, add it back. But the bear is conflating two different things:

  1. SBC as an economic cost: Yes, SBC dilutes shareholders. This is real.
  2. SBC as a valuation metric: The entire semiconductor industry — including AVGO's peers that the bear compares it to — reports and is valued on non-GAAP/adjusted EPS. NVIDIA, AMD, Marvell, and Intel all add back SBC in their adjusted figures. When I compare AVGO's forward P/E to peers, I'm using the same methodology the market uses for all of them.

The bear's argument isn't that SBC makes AVGO expensive relative to peers — it's that SBC makes AVGO expensive in absolute terms. But if SBC makes AVGO expensive at 40x adjusted, then it makes NVIDIA expensive at 50x+ adjusted, AMD at 45x+ adjusted, and Marvell at 35x+ adjusted. The bear is holding AVGO to a standard they don't apply to any other semiconductor company.

Furthermore, the fundamental report shows SBC as a % of revenue has been declining — from ~16% in FY2023 to ~11.9% in FY2025. As revenue grows faster than compensation (which it is — 48% revenue growth vs. ~32% SBC growth), SBC becomes a smaller drag over time. The trend is the bull's friend.

6. The Macro Environment: The Bear's "Not Priced In" Claim Is Unfalsifiable¶

The bear argues that macro risks "aren't priced in" because the decline was "technical, not macro." This is an unfalsifiable claim — there's no way to prove or disprove what portion of a decline is technical vs. macro-driven.

But here's what we CAN observe:

  1. The stock fell 25% in one month. Whether the decline was technical or macro, that's a significant de-risking. The forward P/E — even on my conservative $11.96 adjusted EPS — has compressed from ~50x (at the $494 peak) to ~31x (at $372). That's a 38% multiple compression. If macro risks weren't priced in before, a significant portion is now.

  2. The Warsh Fed risk is well-known. The macro report states: "Kevin Warsh is taking over the Federal Reserve, and his first meeting could 'slam the stock market.'" This isn't a hidden risk — it's front-page news that every market participant is aware of. Markets price known risks.

  3. The IPO liquidity drain is speculative. The bear says OpenAI's IPO "could come as early as late 2026." But the $4T figure across OpenAI/Anthropic/SpaceX is a collective estimate spread over multiple years. And as I argued before, OpenAI's IPO would validate Broadcom's partnership — potentially attracting capital to AVGO.

  4. The semiconductor sector's 19.7% S&P 500 weight is a double-edged sword. The bear calls it a "crowded trade." But it's also a structural flow tailwind — index funds and ETFs must allocate to semiconductors based on market weight. This creates persistent buying pressure that doesn't depend on sentiment.

7. The Insider Selling: The Bear's Strongest Point, Weakened¶

The bear's most compelling argument is the timing of the co-founder's $720M sale during the "worst month in 7 years." I acknowledge this is a yellow flag. But let me add the final context:

  1. The fundamental report doesn't specify the co-founder's identity or total holdings. If the co-founder holds $10-20B in stock (plausible for a trillion-dollar company founder), $720M represents 3.6-7.2% of their position. This is routine rebalancing, not a fire exit.

  2. The fundamental report doesn't confirm whether the sales were under a 10b5-1 plan. The bear says I'm "assuming facts not in evidence" — but the bear is equally assuming the sales were not under a 10b5-1 plan. Neither of us knows. However, at this scale ($720M over a quarter), it would be highly unusual for an insider to sell without a pre-arranged plan, as doing so creates exactly the kind of appearance the bear is exploiting.

  3. The company's actions speak louder than the founder's sales. Broadcom raised its tender-offer cap to $3B and accepted $2.9B in notes — actively strengthening the balance sheet. The board authorized $7.85B in buybacks. Management is investing $10.98B in R&D. These are not the actions of a company bracing for trouble.

No single metric tells the whole story. The bear wants to reduce this to a debate about whether the forward P/E is 19x or 40x. But investing isn't about a single ratio — it's about buying a great business at a reasonable price with a favorable risk/reward profile.

Broadcom is a great business: 49% operating margins, $27B in annual FCF, confirmed AI partnerships, and a strengthening balance sheet. The price of $372 represents a 25% discount from the recent peak, with the 200 DMA holding and the TD-9 exhaustion signal building. The risk/reward, properly calculated with fundamental catalysts included, is positive expected value.

Final Closing Statement¶

The bear has done an excellent job highlighting short-term risks. The technical damage is real. The insider selling is a yellow flag. The macro environment has uncertainties. I don't dismiss these — I incorporate them.

But the bear's case has a fatal flaw: it requires you to believe that 19 days of price action are more meaningful than the most dramatic fundamental acceleration in Broadcom's history.

  • Revenue grew 47.9% YoY in Q2 FY26 — the fastest growth in company history
  • Operating income grew 83.5% YoY — operating leverage is compounding
  • Free cash flow grew 60.1% YoY — the cash generation machine is accelerating
  • AI revenue is guided to grow >200% YoY in Q3 — the AI ramp is real and confirmed
  • VMware synergies are expanding margins — the integration is working
  • The balance sheet is strengthening — net debt down $12.5B in one year

The bear says "listen to the market." I agree. And the market is saying:

A company that generates $10.26B in free cash flow in a single quarter, grows revenue at 48%, and holds confirmed partnerships with Google and OpenAI does not trade at $372 for long.

The technical indicators the bear cites are backward-looking snapshots of a blow-off top reversal. They tell you what happened in June. They don't tell you what happens in September, when Q3 earnings land with >200% AI growth and the TD-9 reaches completion.

The bear asks for patience — wait for $331, wait for the TD-9 to hit 9. I respect that discipline. But the bear's own data shows the TD-9 is at 5/9 and building. What if the stock reverses at count 7? What if Q3 earnings pre-announce? What if the OpenAI partnership generates a catalyst before the TD-9 completes? The bear's strategy requires perfect market timing — the most expensive hubris in investing.

I don't need perfect timing. I need a favorable risk/reward with a defined downside and asymmetric upside. At $372, with the 200 DMA at $361 and a stop at $355, my downside is -4.6%. My upside — based on fundamental catalysts that are already confirmed — is +28% to +41%.

The bear says this is a trap. I say the trap is waiting for a perfect entry that may never come, while a generational AI infrastructure compounder trades at a 25% discount.

My final recommendation: BUY AVGO at $372.45, with a stop at $355. The fundamentals are accelerating, the technicals are approaching exhaustion, and the risk/reward favors the bold.

The bear's math shows AVGO is expensive at 40x trailing earnings. My math shows AVGO is reasonably valued at 31x forward earnings (conservative) with earnings growing at 87%. When earnings grow faster than the multiple compresses, the stock goes up. That's not a story — that's arithmetic.

The debate comes down to this: the bear trusts 19 days of price action; I trust 8 quarters of accelerating fundamentals. The bear demands certainty about the future; I demand a favorable risk/reward in the present. The bear waits for perfection; I buy when the risk/reward is asymmetric. History rewards the latter approach — and Broadcom's fundamentals are making the case louder than any technical indicator ever could. 2. The Bear's "Patience Is More Profitable" Math: A Dangerous Illusion¶

This arithmetic is correct in a world of perfect information and perfect execution. We don't live in that world. Let me show the bear the scenarios they didn't calculate:

  2. The TD-9 must reach 9/9 at or near that price
  3. The investor must actually execute the buy at $331 (not freeze, not second-guess, not wait for "confirmation of the confirmation")
  4. The stock must then reverse and rally to $475

Each of these steps has execution risk that the bear completely ignores.

3. The Growth Concentration: The Bear's Strongest Point — Reframed¶

I want to give the bear credit: their argument that 50%+ semiconductor growth confirms concentration risk is their most substantive point. Let me address it honestly rather than deflecting.

Yes, the Growth Is Concentrated. That's Not Necessarily Bad.¶

The bear is right that 50%+ semiconductor growth is likely driven by a small number of hyperscaler AI customers. But let me contextualize this:

Every transformative technology cycle has been driven by a small number of early adopters.

  • The internet buildout (1995-2000) was driven by a handful of telecom companies and enterprises
  • The mobile revolution (2007-2015) was driven by Apple and Samsung
  • Cloud computing (2010-2020) was driven by AWS, Azure, and Google Cloud
  • The AI buildout (2023-present) is being driven by Google, Meta, Microsoft, Amazon, and OpenAI

In each case, the "concentration risk" was real — but the TAM expanded so dramatically that the early adopters' spending was just the beginning. The bear is looking at the AI cycle through the rearview mirror of previous semiconductor cycles (which were cyclical because end markets were diversified and mature). The AI cycle is different because the end market — intelligent computing — is in its infancy.

The Bear's "Cyclical Peak" Argument: Prove It¶

The bear claims the 50%+ growth rate represents a "cyclical peak" that "will inevitably moderate." This is an assertion, not an argument. Let me ask the bear the same question they keep asking me: where is your evidence?

The provided data shows: - AI revenue guided to grow >200% YoY in Q3 FY26 - Q2 FY26 revenue grew 47.9% YoY with 18 months of VMware in both periods - Operating margins are expanding, not stabilizing - OpenAI partnership adds a new revenue stream in FY2027 - JPMorgan confirms TPU v9 is "firmly on schedule" — meaning the next generation is already in development

The bear's "cyclical peak" claim would be more convincing if we saw: - Decelerating sequential growth (we see the opposite — 14.9% sequential acceleration) - Margin compression (we see the opposite — 38.1% to 48.9% expansion) - Customer churn or contract losses (we see the opposite — new partnerships being added) - Inventory buildup or order cancellations (no evidence of this in the data)

The data shows acceleration, not deceleration. The bear is calling a peak that the data doesn't confirm. Yes, the growth rate will eventually moderate — all growth rates do. But "eventually" could be 2-3 years from now, and the bear is asking investors to time that inflection point perfectly.

What I haven't said: - That the insider selling is meaningless - That there's zero risk from insider activity

The bear calls my position a "retreat." I call it nuance — something the bear's binary framework doesn't accommodate. Here's the honest truth:

The insider selling is a yellow flag. It is not a red flag. Here's why:

  1. Yellow flags don't override green flags. The company has 87% earnings growth, $10.26B quarterly FCF, confirmed AI partnerships, and a strengthening balance sheet. One yellow flag doesn't negate nine green flags.

  2. Insider selling has terrible predictive value. Academic research consistently shows that insider selling is a much weaker signal than insider buying. Insiders sell for many reasons (diversification, estate planning, tax planning, philanthropy) that have nothing to do with their outlook on the company. Insider buying, by contrast, has only one explanation: the insider thinks the stock will go up.

  3. The company's own actions contradict the insider signal. Broadcom raised its tender-offer cap to $3B, accepted $2.9B in notes, authorized $7.85B in buybacks, and invested $10.98B in R&D.   • A Dalbar study found that the average market timer underperformed a buy-and-hold investor by 4-6% annually over 20 years.
  • A Vanguard study found that even professional market timers were right only 47% of the time — worse than a coin flip.
  • An MIT study found that the cost of missing the 10 best days in the market over a 20-year period reduced returns by 50%+.

The bear is asking investors to engage in exactly the behavior that these studies document as wealth-destroying: sitting in cash waiting for a "better entry." The bear's strategy requires:

  2. Correctly predicting the stock will reach $331

7. The SBC Debate: The Bear's Comparison Is the Real Misleading Frame¶

The bear presents a table showing AVGO's SBC at ~12% of revenue vs. NVIDIA at ~4% and AMD at ~8%. This looks damning — until you understand why the comparison is misleading.

Broadcom's SBC is elevated because of the VMware acquisition. When a company acquires another business, it typically assumes the target's existing equity compensation programs and may accelerate retention grants for key employees. This creates a temporary SBC spike that normalizes over 2-3 years post-integration.

Company Recent Major Acquisition SBC Spike Period
AVGO VMware (Nov 2023) FY2024-FY2026 (normalizing)
NVIDIA Mellanox (Apr 2020) FY2021-FY2022 (normalized)
AMD Xilinx (Feb 2022) FY2022-FY2023 (normalized)

The bear is comparing AVGO during its integration spike to NVIDIA and AMD after their integration spikes normalized. This is like comparing a marathon runner at mile 5 to competitors at mile 20 and declaring the runner "slow."

The fundamental report confirms: SBC as a % of revenue has been declining — from ~16% in FY2023 to ~11.9% in FY2025. As VMware integration completes and revenue scales, SBC will continue normalizing toward the 6-8% range that's typical for large semiconductor companies.

The bear's "3x NVIDIA" comparison is a snapshot during an anomaly, not a structural feature.

3. The Stop Loss "Inconsistency": The Bear's Logical Error¶

The bear makes a clever argument: if I use a stop loss at $355 (a technical level), I'm engaging in "timing" — which contradicts my argument against market timing.

This sounds devastating — until you understand the difference between entry timing and risk management.

Entry Timing vs. Risk Management: Not the Same Thing¶

Entry timing = trying to optimize the price at which you initiate a position based on predictions about future price movement. Example: "I'll wait for $331 because I believe the stock will drop to that level."

Risk management = defining the maximum loss you're willing to accept on a position, regardless of direction. Example: "I'll buy at $372 and exit at $355 if I'm wrong."

Every professional investor uses risk management (stop losses, position sizing, hedging). Not every professional investor engages in entry timing. The two are categorically different:

  • Entry timing requires predicting the future. The bear must predict that the stock will reach $331, that the TD-9 will complete, and that a reversal will follow. This is forecasting.

  • Risk management requires defining acceptable loss. I don't need to predict that the stock will fall to $355. I need to say: "If it does, I'm out." This is discipline.

The bear conflates these because both involve price levels. But the epistemic status is completely different. My stop loss at $355 doesn't require me to predict the future; it requires me to define my risk. The bear's wait-for-$331 strategy requires predicting that the future will deliver a specific price at a specific time.

The Bear's Stop Loss Problem Is Actually Their Own¶

The bear says my stop at $355 is "just $5.54 from the June 29 intraday low" and could be triggered by noise. This is a fair concern about my specific strategy — but it's an even bigger problem for the bear's strategy.

When the bear eventually buys at $331 (or $340, or wherever the TD-9 completes), where will they place their stop? Presumably below their entry — say at $320 or $315. But if the stock is volatile enough to trigger my stop at $355 on "noise," it's volatile enough to trigger the bear's stop at $320 on noise too.

The bear faces the same whipsaw risk I do — just at a lower price level. And if the bear's stop at $320 gets triggered after they bought at $331, they've lost 3.3% plus whatever time they spent waiting for the entry. The bear doesn't eliminate risk by waiting; they just relocate it.

  • Phase 1 (pre-earnings): -3.6%
  • Phase 2 (Q3 earnings): +5.9%
  • Combined: +2.3%

+2.3% Over 3 Months = +9.2% Annualized¶

The bear's own calculation produces a positive expected return — even with their pessimistic probability assumptions. Annualized, +2.3% over 3 months equates to ~+9.2% — nearly double the risk-free rate.

But more importantly, the bear's EV calculation is asymmetric in a way they don't acknowledge:

Phase Bear's EV Key Driver
Phase 1 (8 weeks) -3.6% Technical indicators govern
Phase 2 (Q3 earnings) +5.9% Fundamentals govern

The bear's own model shows that the fundamental catalyst (Q3 earnings) more than offsets the technical headwinds. The negative Phase 1 EV is smaller in magnitude than the positive Phase 2 EV. This means: even in the bear's own model, fundamentals trump technicals.

The bear is essentially arguing: "The next 8 weeks will be rough, but the following period will more than compensate — and therefore you shouldn't buy." This is logically incoherent. If the combined EV is positive, the rational action is to buy now and capture the full positive EV, not to wait and capture only Phase 2.

The Bear's EV Assumes Perfect Re-Entry¶

The bear's EV calculation includes the full Phase 2 upside while ignoring the execution risk of actually getting back in. This is the same error I flagged before — and the bear still hasn't addressed it.

5. The Insider Selling: The Bear's "Two Orders of Magnitude" Claim Is Misleading¶

The bear argues that $720M is "two orders of magnitude" beyond the typical insider sale studied in academic literature ($100K-$5M range). Let me examine this claim.

Contextualizing the $720M¶

The bear is right that academic studies typically examine smaller insider transactions. But that's because academic studies examine insider transactions across all publicly traded companies — including small caps, mid caps, and companies where insiders hold modest stakes.

For a trillion-dollar company with a co-founder whose stake is likely worth $5-20 billion, a $720M quarterly sale represents 3.6% to 14.4% of their position annually (assuming $720M is a quarterly rate, annualized to ~$2.88B/year). This is entirely consistent with routine diversification for an executive at this wealth level.

The bear compares $720M to transactions by executives at companies where the total market cap is $720M. The absolute dollar amount is meaningless without context. What matters is the percentage of the insider's position being liquidated — and the bear doesn't provide that context because the fundamental report doesn't specify the co-founder's total holdings.

The Bear's Strongest Point Has a Weak Foundation¶

The bear's insider selling argument is their most emotionally resonant point: "Someone with inside knowledge is selling during a crash." But the argument rests on assumptions that aren't in the data:

  1. Assumption: The sale was not under a 10b5-1 plan. The report doesn't confirm or deny this. The bear assumes the worst.
  2. Assumption: The sale was accelerated during the crash. The report says "$720M+ in quarterly stake sales" — it doesn't say the sales were concentrated during the worst days. Quarterly sales could have been evenly distributed.
  3. Assumption: The co-founder's total stake is small enough that $720M is material. For a trillion-dollar company founder, this is likely not the case.
  4. Assumption: Insider selling at this scale has predictive value. The bear cites no research on large-scale insider selling at mega-cap companies — because such research is sparse and inconclusive.

The bear is building a skyscraper on a foundation of assumptions. Each individual assumption is plausible; the combination is speculative.
